\section{The question, its provenance, and the advance}\label{gam:sec:context}
Athanasiadis and Chapoton associate a support polynomial to every finite
preorder and ask whether it has nonnegative coefficients in the gamma basis
$t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}$ \cite[Conjecture~5.2]{AC}. The question is stronger than
palindromicity and unimodality: positive combinations of these centered
binomial polynomials have both properties, but a symmetric unimodal
polynomial need not be gamma-positive. It is weaker than real-rootedness.
Their separate Conjecture~5.1(d) asks for a flag simplicial-polytope
realization; that is a geometric statement, not a reformulation of
gamma-positivity (manuscript~01).

Part~II of this report provides the immediate research context. At the
snapshot manuscript~06 inspected, Part~II proves gamma-positivity for posets
of height at most two. Its explicit open boundary includes arbitrary height
and height-two quotient posets whose elements have been replaced by
nontrivial equivalence classes. The main result below removes both
restrictions. It also identifies every gamma coefficient by a finite
matching condition. The answer to Conjecture~5.2 is affirmative. The word
``supports'' is essential: a pair of vertex sets is counted once whenever at
least one suitable matching exists, regardless of how many such matchings it
has. Likewise, ``disjoint'' refers to the underlying elements of the
preorder, not merely to left and right copies of a bipartite graph
(manuscript~01).

\subsection{What is inherited, and what is proved here}
The matching-support interpretation for augmented bipartite root polytopes
is prior work of Ohsugi and Tsuchiya \cite[Propositions~3.3--3.4]{OT20},
restated by Davis and Kohl \cite[Theorem~3.10]{DK}. The needed total-count
identity follows with the hypertree interpretation of root-polytope volume
\cite{KP}; Part~II already uses this identity in its height-two
argument (Lemma~\ref{htwo:lem:count}). None of these facts is claimed as new.

Dai, Hou, Liu, Thawinrak, and Wang identify bipartite demand support
polynomials with the corresponding root-polytope numerators and prove
preorder duality \cite[Theorems~1.2--1.3]{DHLTW}. Menon gives a bijective
proof of gamma-positivity for octopuses and an extension to lopsided
octopuses \cite{Menon}. These are relevant antecedents, not results that
this addition claims to discover. The theorem below includes Menon's
families and all other finite preorders; their explicit special-case
formulas are recoveries (Section~\ref{gam:sec:extremal}), and univariate
duality is recovered and refined rather than claimed
(Theorem~\ref{gam:gv:thm:duality}).

The additional argument is the universal support-fibre reduction in
Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:fibre}, together with its Boolean summation. It applies to
\emph{every} finite preorder. The cancellation theorem gives another
explanation, and the endpoint-defect theorem identifies exactly when
palindromicity occurs in the balanced perfect-matching setting. The block,
extremal, matching-deficiency, and probabilistic formulas make the resulting
structure usable beyond the original conjecture.

\subsection{Precise scope of the conclusions}
The theorem settles the statement of Conjecture~5.2 in the version of
\cite{AC} consulted here; it also proves its asserted palindromicity and
unimodality consequences. Part~I's ordinary simplicial-polytopal
realization is not an input. In particular, no unrefereed geometric assertion
in ProveIt is needed to complete the proof.

The stronger flag-polytopal realization question is not settled. Nor does
gamma-positivity imply real-rootedness: Part~II records and extends
previously posted counterexamples to the latter conjecture, with priority
credited there to Shivam Patel~\cite{Patel}. We do not re-claim those
counterexamples. We also do not identify $h_\tau$ with the Ehrhart numerator
of the \emph{original} preorder polytope; those are different invariants.

The ProveIt tree inspected by manuscript~06 is pinned to commit

\small\texttt{9b24a3a8d545af9624f6ac455f5b548be62818b6}.
\end{center}

\subsection{The repository frontier seen by manuscript~01}
The ProveIt snapshot inspected by manuscript~01 is commit
\begin{center}
\small\texttt{077672c84f743ff5de82ce6becc3ad6b2ff8787a}.
\end{center}
Its report \emph{A Reflexive Root-Polytope Model for Preorder
$h$-Polynomials}, in
\begin{center}\small
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/enumerative-combinatorics/}\par
\nolinkurl{preorder-root-polytopes/}
\end{center}
contains a height-two theorem in Part~II and later cactus results in
Part~III. [Merge note: that is this report, Parts~I--III as printed above.]
Part~II expresses the gamma coefficients of a height-at-most-two
\emph{poset} through perfectly matchable subsets of its bipartite
comparability graph. Its further-research section explicitly asks for an
extension to greater height and to nontrivial preorder-equivalence blocks
(Research question~\ref{gam:q:extend}). The README at that snapshot still
distinguishes this restricted theorem from the unsettled general
gamma-positivity question.

The new observation is that one should partition by \emph{all} zero
coordinates, not only by zeros on a minimal shore. For a support $S$ with
complement $Z$, excess demand at any element of $S$ can be supplied from
comparable elements of $Z$. Transitivity turns the ideal inequalities into
the appropriate Hall inequalities. This construction applies unchanged to
arbitrary height and to supports which split equivalence classes.

Part~II supplies the useful demand-counting lemma by a reduction to
classical root-polytope results. Manuscript~01 reproduces the reduction with
its published dependencies identified. Therefore the general theorem does
not require accepting Part~I's unrefereed geometric claims.

\section{Definitions and main theorems}\label{gam:sec:main}
Let $E$ be a finite set of size $n$. A preorder $\tau$ is a reflexive,
transitive relation $\le_\tau$ on $E$. We do not assume antisymmetry. An
\emph{ideal} is a subset $I$ such that $v\in I$ and $u\le_\tau v$ imply
$u\in I$. Write $\J$ for all ideals, including $\varnothing$ and $E$.
For $T\subseteq E$, put
\[
 \down T=\{u\in E:u\le_\tau v\text{ for some }v\in T\}.
\]
Reflexivity gives $T\subseteq\down T$, and transitivity makes $\down T$ an
ideal. These are exactly the two properties used in the main reduction.

For $x\in\R^E$ and $I\subseteq E$, write $x(I)=\sum_{i\in I}x_i$.
The preorder polytope and its support polynomials are, as in Part~I,
\begin{align}
 \Qt&=\{x\in\R_{\ge0}^{E}:x(I)\le |I|\text{ for every }I\in\J\},
 \label{gam:eq:Q}\\
 h_\tau(t)&=\sum_{x\in\Qt\cap\Z^E}t^{|\supp(x)|},
 \qquad \supp(x)=\{i:x_i>0\},\label{gam:eq:h}\\
 H_\tau(\zv)&=\sum_{x\in\Qt\cap\Z^E}z_{\supp(x)},\qquad
 z_A=\prod_{i\in A}z_i,\qquad h_\tau(t)=H_\tau(t,\ldots,t).\label{gam:gv:eq:H}
\end{align}
The full-set inequality makes the lattice-point set finite. The all-zero
point is the only point of empty support, and the all-one point is the only
point of full support, since its $n$ positive integer coordinates already sum
to at least $n$. Thus $h_\tau$ has degree $n$ and both endpoint
coefficients are one.

The polynomial $h_\tau$ is initially a \emph{support enumerator}. It must not
be confused with the Ehrhart numerator $h^*(\Qt;t)$. An Ehrhart numerator
of a different root polytope appears in Section~\ref{gam:gv:sec:orbits}
(manuscript~01).

\begin{definition}[Disjoint transport supports]\label{gam:def:cores}
Let $\Tset_k(\tau)$ be the set of ordered pairs $(U,V)$ of subsets of $E$ such
that
\[
 U\cap V=\varnothing,\qquad |U|=|V|=k,
\]
and there exists a bijection $f:U\to V$ satisfying $u\le_\tau f(u)$ for every
$u\in U$. Set $\Tset(\tau)=\bigcup_k\Tset_k(\tau)$. More generally, for
subsets $A,B\subseteq E$, write $A\match_\tau B$ if $|A|=|B|$ and there
exists a bijection $f:A\to B$ satisfying $a\le_\tau f(a)$ for every $a\in A$
(manuscript~01's ``compatible support pairs''; $A$ and $B$ may overlap).
\end{definition}
The empty pair is included. A support pair with several possible bijections
is counted once. The order of the pair matters, especially inside a
nontrivial equivalence class.

\begin{theorem}[General preorder gamma formula; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:thm:main}
For every finite preorder $\tau$ on $E$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 h_\tau(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}|\Tset_k(\tau)|\,
                  t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}.}\label{gam:eq:main}
\end{equation}
More strongly, for commuting variables $\zv=(z_i)_{i\in E}$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 H_\tau(\zv)=\sum_{x\in\Qt\cap\Z^E}z_{\supp(x)}
   =\sum_{(U,V)\in\Tset(\tau)}z_V\!
          \prod_{i\in E\setminus(U\cup V)}(1+z_i).}\label{gam:eq:multi}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\gamma_k(\tau)=|\Tset_k(\tau)|$ are nonnegative integers, proving
Athanasiadis--Chapoton Conjecture~5.2 for arbitrary finite preorders.
\end{theorem}
Here and below a gamma expansion is a polynomial identity; no value is
assigned to a rational substitution at its pole $t=-1$. The expansion is
unique: $t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}$ has lowest degree $k$ with coefficient one, so
successive coefficients in increasing degree determine
$\gamma_0,\gamma_1,\ldots$ (manuscript~01). Define the gamma polynomial
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_k\gamma_k(\tau)u^k.
 \label{gam:eq:gammapoly}
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{gam:eq:main} can be abbreviated as
$h_\tau(t)=(1+t)^n\Gamma_\tau(t/(1+t)^2)$, with the right side interpreted
by its polynomial expansion, not by substitution at the undefined quotient
$t=-1$. The degree of $\Gamma_\tau$ and the multiplicity of $-1$ as a root
of $h_\tau$ are determined exactly in Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:deficiency}, which
both manuscripts state (manuscript~01 already in its introduction): with
$\Comp(\tau)$ the simple undirected graph on $E$ in which distinct vertices
are adjacent precisely when they are comparable in at least one direction,
$\deg\Gamma_\tau=\nu(\Comp(\tau))$ and $\gamma_k(\tau)>0$ exactly for
$0\le k\le\nu(\Comp(\tau))$.

There is a second result about a relation which need not be transitive.
For a reflexive relation $R$ on $E$, form the bipartite graph $G_R$ on two
copies of $E$, with edge $i_Lj_R$ exactly when $iRj$; for a preorder,
$G_\tau$ has edge $a_Lb_R$ exactly when $a\le_\tau b$. Define
\begin{equation}
 p_R(t)=\sum_{\substack{A,B\subseteq E,\ |A|=|B|\\
                         G_R[A_L,B_R]\text{ has a perfect matching}}}
                 t^{|A|}.
 \label{gam:eq:pR}
\end{equation}
Its degree is $n$, since all diagonal edges are present. The relation
$\overline R$ denotes reflexive transitive closure, and $|R|$ counts ordered
related pairs, including the $n$ diagonal pairs. [Merge note: $G_\tau$ is
Part~I's $G_\tau$ with its shores exchanged, and $p_\tau=p_{G_\tau}$ is the
polynomial of Part~II's Remark~\ref{htwo:rem:doubled}; see
Table~\ref{gam:tab:notation}.]

\begin{theorem}[Transitivity is the palindromicity criterion; manuscript~06]\label{gam:thm:graphintro}
For $n\ge1$ and every reflexive relation $R$,
\begin{equation}
 [t]p_R(t)=|R|,\qquad [t^{n-1}]p_R(t)=|\overline R|.
 \label{gam:eq:endpoints}
\end{equation}
The following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt]
\item $R$ is transitive;
\item every matchable pair $(A,B)$ remains matchable after removing $A\cap B$;
\item $p_R(t)$ is palindromic of degree $n$;
\item $p_R(t)$ is gamma-positive of degree $n$;
\item $[t^{n-1}]p_R(t)=[t]p_R(t)$.
\end{enumerate}
In particular, the asymmetry between these two coefficients counts exactly
the ordered pairs missing from transitive closure:
\begin{equation}
 [t^{n-1}]p_R-[t]p_R=|\overline R\setminus R|.
 \label{gam:eq:defect}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
For $n=0$, all statements except the displayed coefficient indices have the
obvious trivial interpretation. The theorem's proof in
Sections~\ref{gam:sec:cancel} and~\ref{gam:sec:graph} is elementary and does
not use root polytopes or Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:main}.

\section{The established bipartite counting identity}\label{gam:sec:count}
We state the non-elementary enumerative input precisely and record its
reduction to the cited classical theorems. This also fixes the distinction
between support counts, matching counts, and hypertree counts.

Let $H=(X\sqcup Y,F)$ be a finite bipartite graph with specified shores, and
let $N_H(T)\subseteq X$ be the neighborhood of $T\subseteq Y$. Its integral
demand set is Part~II's
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal R_H=\{c\in\N^Y:c(T)\le |N_H(T)|\text{ for all }T\subseteq Y\}.
 \label{gam:eq:demands}
\end{equation}
A vector in $\mathcal R_H$ describes integer requests at right vertices that
can be served by distinct unit-capacity left vertices; we count demand
\emph{vectors}, not allocations serving them. Write $\mathcal M(H)$ for the
pairs $(U,V)$ with $U\subseteq X$, $V\subseteq Y$, $|U|=|V|$, such that
$H[U,V]$ has a perfect matching, and
$p_H(u)=\sum_{(U,V)\in\mathcal M(H)}u^{|V|}$, the perfectly matchable set
polynomial; the specified shores allow us to write a matchable vertex set
uniquely as $U\sqcup V$.

\begin{lemma}[Demand/support equinumeracy, inherited]\label{gam:lem:count}
For every finite bipartite graph $H$,
\begin{equation}
 |\mathcal R_H|=|\mathcal M(H)|=p_H(1).
 \label{gam:eq:count}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
This is Lemma~\ref{htwo:lem:count} of Part~II. Both manuscripts prove it by
the reduction printed there: adjoin $x_0$, $y_0$ with all edges $x_0y$,
$xy_0$, $x_0y_0$ to obtain the connected graph $\widehat H$; combine
\eqref{htwo:eq:KPinput} (K\'alm\'an--Postnikov~\cite{KP}, evaluated at one;
manuscript~01 cites its Theorems~1.1 and~3.10) with \eqref{htwo:eq:OTinput}
(Ohsugi--Tsuchiya~\cite[Propositions~3.3--3.4]{OT20}, in the augmented form
of Davis--Kohl~\cite[Theorem~3.10]{DK}) through $h^*(B;1)=\Vol(B)$, with the
full affine integer lattice; and identify the right hypertree vectors with
$\mathcal R_H$ by the forest count and the clone-and-Hall construction of
Section~\ref{htwo:sec:count}. The manuscripts' versions differ from Part~II's
only in presentation: manuscript~06 bounds the forest on
$T\cup N_H(T)\cup\{x_0\}$ directly, and manuscript~01 spells out the
augmenting-path proof of the required direction of Hall's criterion (choose
a largest matching; absence of an augmenting path from an unmatched clone
yields a reached clone set larger than its neighborhood). Both note that
empty shores give the single empty matching support, and manuscript~01 that
the construction need not choose a unique tree, because the resulting
\emph{vector} is unique. Manuscript~01 adds: the augmentation matters;
identity~\eqref{htwo:eq:OTinput} is about the edge polytope of $\widehat H$,
not the unaugmented edge polytope of $H$ and not a polytope whose vertices
are individual matchings.

\begin{remark}[The input is not a matching enumeration; manuscript~06]\label{gam:rem:K22}
For $H=K_{2,2}$, there are six demand vectors: the nonnegative pairs of total
at most two. There are also six matchable support pairs: the empty pair,
four single-edge supports, and one full support. There are two perfect
matchings on the full support, but it contributes only one. Replacing
$|\mathcal M(H)|$ by the number of individual matchings would already fail
in this example.
\end{remark}

\subsection{A support-resolved version by Boolean inversion}
For later use, the total identity also determines every prescribed right
support. This refinement recovers the known support-enumerator identity;
it is not a new priority claim for that identity. It requires no additional
geometric theorem.

\begin{proposition}[Prescribed demand support; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:prop:gradedinput}
For every $S\subseteq Y$,
\begin{equation}
 \#\{c\in\mathcal R_H:\supp(c)=S\}
  =\#\{U\subseteq X:(U,S)\in\mathcal M(H)\}.
 \label{gam:eq:gradedinput}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 F_H(\zv):=\sum_{c\in\mathcal R_H}z_{\supp(c)}
       =\sum_{(U,V)\in\mathcal M(H)}z_V.
 \label{gam:eq:FH}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $T\subseteq Y$, the demands supported inside $T$ are exactly those for
$H[X,T]$: inequalities involving outside vertices follow from the ones on
$T$, since outside coordinates are zero and neighborhoods only enlarge.
By Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:count}, their number is
\[
 a(T)=\#\{(U,V)\in\mathcal M(H):V\subseteq T\}.
\]
Both sides of \eqref{gam:eq:gradedinput}, as functions of $S$, therefore have
the same sums over all $S\subseteq T$. Boolean inversion gives their
equality. Explicitly, a fixed $V\subseteq S$ contributes
\[
 \sum_{V\subseteq T\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S|-|T|}
 =\begin{cases}1&V=S,\\0&V\ne S.\end{cases}
\]
Summing \eqref{gam:eq:gradedinput} against $z_S$ proves \eqref{gam:eq:FH}.
\end{proof}
Manuscript~01 proves the same refinement by induction on $|T|$ and records
the explicit value: both sides of~\eqref{gam:eq:gradedinput} equal
$\sum_{V\subseteq S}(-1)^{|S|-|V|}p_{H[X\sqcup V]}(1)$.

No explicit canonical bijection between the two sets in
\eqref{gam:eq:gradedinput} has been constructed in this argument. The
equality is enough for all the polynomial identities below.

\begin{remark}[What is not constructed; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:rem:notconstructed}
The lemmas give equality of cardinalities, refined by the receiver support.
They do not specify a natural bijection from a demand vector to a matchable
support pair. The explicit tree construction is a bijection with hypertree
\emph{vectors}, not with matchings or with the pairs counted by $p_H$.
This distinction remains important when we introduce Boolean actions
(Section~\ref{gam:gv:sec:orbits}).
\end{remark}

\section{Proof of the general preorder theorem}\label{gam:sec:proof}
The decisive step is to apply the bipartite identity to the zeros and the
positive coordinates of an arbitrary preorder point, rather than to fixed
minimal and maximal levels of a height-two poset.

\begin{lemma}[Every support fibre is a bipartite demand set; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:lem:fibre}
Fix $S\subseteq E$ and set $Z=E\setminus S$. Let $H_{Z,S}$ be the bipartite
graph with shores $Z,S$ and edges $zs$ exactly when $z\le_\tau s$. The map
\begin{equation}
 c\longmapsto x,\qquad x_z=0\ (z\in Z),\quad x_s=1+c_s\ (s\in S)
 \label{gam:eq:fibremap}
\end{equation}
is a bijection from $\mathcal R_{H_{Z,S}}$ onto the lattice points of
$\Qt$ with support exactly $S$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The map is plainly a bijection between $\N^S$ and all nonnegative integral
vectors of support $S$. We prove the equivalence of its feasibility
conditions. For an ideal $I$, the preorder inequality is
\begin{equation}
 c(I\cap S)\le |I\cap Z|.
 \label{gam:eq:idealexcess}
\end{equation}
Suppose these inequalities hold. Given $T\subseteq S$, take the ideal
$I=\down T$. Then $T\subseteq I\cap S$, and $c$ is nonnegative, so
\[
 c(T)\le c((\down T)\cap S)
       \le |(\down T)\cap Z|=|N_{H_{Z,S}}(T)|.
\]
Thus $c\in\mathcal R_{H_{Z,S}}$.

Conversely, suppose all the displayed neighborhood bounds hold. For an
arbitrary ideal $I$, put $T=I\cap S$. Downward closure gives
$\down T\subseteq I$, whence
\[
 c(I\cap S)\le |(\down (I\cap S))\cap Z|\le |I\cap Z|.
\]
This is \eqref{gam:eq:idealexcess}, so $x\in\Qt$. Both implications use only
reflexivity, transitivity, and nonnegativity. They do not use a height bound,
or require equivalence classes to be singletons.
\end{proof}
Manuscript~01 calls this the all-zero-set reduction and adds: no property of
a minimal or maximal layer is used. In particular, the set $Z$ need not be an
ideal, the set $S$ need not be a filter, and neither set need be a union of
equivalence classes. The only order-theoretic facts used are that $\down T$
is an ideal and that an ideal contains the lower closure of each of its
subsets.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:main}]
For each $S\subseteq E$, Lemmas~\ref{gam:lem:fibre} and~\ref{gam:lem:count} give
\begin{equation}
 \#\{x\in\Qt\cap\Z^E:\supp(x)=S\}
   =\#\{(U,V)\in\Tset(\tau):U\subseteq E\setminus S,\ V\subseteq S\}.
 \label{gam:eq:fixedsupport}
\end{equation}
Indeed, the pairs on the right are exactly the matchable support pairs in
$H_{E\setminus S,S}$. Multiply by $z_S$ and sum over $S$; all sums are
finite, so we may sum first over the pairs. For a fixed
$(U,V)\in\Tset(\tau)$, the permissible supports are precisely
\[
 V\subseteq S\subseteq E\setminus U.
\]
Every such $S$ is uniquely $V\cup W$ with
$W\subseteq E\setminus(U\cup V)$. Its total contribution is
\[
 \sum_{W\subseteq E\setminus(U\cup V)}z_Vz_W
   =z_V\prod_{i\in E\setminus(U\cup V)}(1+z_i).
\]
This proves \eqref{gam:eq:multi}. Specializing all $z_i$ to $t$ gives
\eqref{gam:eq:main}, because $|U|=|V|=k$ leaves exactly $n-2k$ free labels.
\end{proof}
Equation~\eqref{gam:eq:fixedsupport} is manuscript~01's Corollary
``Support-by-support counting formula'': the general theorem is not merely an
equality after forgetting which coordinates are nonzero.

\begin{example}[Why equivalence classes cause no exception; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:ex:equiv3}
If $E=\{1,2,3\}$ is a single equivalence class, the only ideals are
$\varnothing$ and $E$. The lattice point $(3,0,0)$ is allowed. Its support
$S=\{1\}$ is not a union of equivalence classes, but the reduction still
works: the two zero coordinates supply the excess demand $c_1=2$ through
the two edges of $H_{Z,S}$. Nothing requires an individual coordinate to be
bounded by one. In this example $h_\tau=1+9t+9t^2+t^3$ and
$\Gamma_\tau=1+6u$.
\end{example}

\subsection{What the Boolean expansion does and does not provide}
Equation~\eqref{gam:eq:fixedsupport} says that each lattice-point support count
is the number of abstract intervals
$[V,E\setminus U]$ containing that support, with $(U,V)$ a transport pair.
An interval has bottom rank $k$, top rank $n-k$, and rank enumerator
$t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}$. This is the source of gamma-positivity.

The intervals are an \emph{enumerative model}; they are not asserted to be
actual coordinatewise subposets of the lattice-point set. Manuscript~06
constructs no commuting involutions on those lattice points and no canonical
partition of them into geometric cubes. Those stronger structures would
require a compatible, explicit realization of the counting identity in
Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:count}. (Manuscript~01's commuting toggles,
Theorem~\ref{gam:gv:thm:orbits}, act on a support-pair model, not on the
lattice points.)

\begin{corollary}[Palindromicity and strict unimodality; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:cor:unimodal}
For every finite preorder, $h_\tau(t)=t^nh_\tau(t^{-1})$, that is,
$h_j(\tau)=h_{n-j}(\tau)$, and
$h_0(\tau)\le h_1(\tau)\le\cdots\le h_{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}(\tau)$ (manuscript~06).
In fact the coefficients are strictly increasing up to the middle
(manuscript~01):
\[
 [t^j]h_\tau<[t^{j+1}]h_\tau
                 \quad(0\le j<\lfloor n/2\rfloor).
\]
When $n$ is odd, the two middle coefficients are equal. The inequalities on
the other side follow by symmetry.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Each polynomial $t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}$ has symmetric coefficients centered at
$n/2$, nondecreasing up to that center. A nonnegative sum preserves both
properties. There is no appeal to a geometric realization. Moreover
$\gamma_0=1$, and $(1+t)^n$ has strictly increasing coefficients for the
indicated indices. Adding the remaining nonnegative symmetric-unimodal
summands preserves these strict inequalities.
\end{proof}
Thus the theorem proves the palindromicity and unimodality assertions of
Athanasiadis--Chapoton Conjecture~5.1(a)--(b) as well as Conjecture~5.2.
It makes no implication in the reverse direction: a palindromic unimodal
polynomial need not be gamma-positive. [Merge note: Part~I proposes these
properties, and more, through its simplicial polytope $S_\tau$
(Theorem~\ref{thm:main}); the proof here is independent of Part~I's
unrefereed geometric argument.]

\section{Ideal inequalities, cancellation, and a second proof}\label{gam:sec:cancel}
The gamma coefficients can be recognized without searching through
bijections. This also explains why transitivity is the exact structural
hypothesis behind the Boolean intervals.

\begin{lemma}[The ideal Hall criterion; manuscript~06]\label{gam:lem:idealhall}
For subsets $A,B\subseteq E$ of equal size, there is a bijection
$f:A\to B$ with $a\le_\tau f(a)$ if and only if
\begin{equation}
 |A\cap I|\ge |B\cap I|\qquad\text{for every }I\in\J.
 \label{gam:eq:idealhall}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If $b\in B\cap I$, its matched predecessor belongs to $A\cap I$. Distinct
$b$ have distinct predecessors, proving necessity. Conversely, let
$T\subseteq B$. The predecessors of $T$ inside $A$ are $A\cap\down T$.
Since $\down T$ is an ideal and $T\subseteq B\cap\down T$,
\[
 |T|\le |B\cap\down T|\le |A\cap\down T|.
\]
Hall's criterion gives a matching saturating $B$; equality of the shore
sizes makes it a bijection.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Common-support cancellation; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:cor:cancel}
For any $A,B\subseteq E$ with $|A|=|B|$, the pair $(A,B)$ is matchable in
$G_\tau$ if and only if $(A\setminus B,B\setminus A)$ is matchable; that is,
\begin{equation}
 A\match_\tau B
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
 A\setminus B\match_\tau B\setminus A.
 \label{gam:eq:cancellation}
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The common intersection contributes the same quantity to both sides of
\eqref{gam:eq:idealhall}, so it cancels. Alternatively, the reverse implication
extends a matching by the diagonal edges on $A\cap B$.
\end{proof}

Manuscript~01 states~\eqref{gam:eq:cancellation} as its Lemma ``Canceling
common support'' and proves it without the ideal Hall criterion.

\begin{proof}[Second proof of Corollary~\ref{gam:cor:cancel}, by path decomposition (manuscript~01)]
Suppose first that $f:A\to B$ is a compatible bijection. On the original
ground set draw the directed edges $a\to f(a)$, retaining possible loops.
Every vertex has indegree and outdegree at most one. A vertex in
$U=A\setminus B$ has outdegree one and indegree zero; one in
$V=B\setminus A$ has indegree one and outdegree zero; one in $A\cap B$ has
both degrees one. The nontrivial components are therefore directed paths
from $U$ to $V$ and directed cycles lying entirely in $A\cap B$.

To justify that a path beginning in $U$ cannot enter a cycle, the first entry
vertex of that cycle would receive two incoming edges. Since the graph is
finite, the path must instead terminate, and it can terminate only in $V$.
Different starting vertices yield disjoint paths. Along each path,
transitivity implies that its starting vertex is below its ending vertex.
Replacing every path by this direct relation gives a bijection from $U$ to
$V$. Cycles and loops are discarded.

Conversely, a compatible bijection from $U$ to $V$ extends to one from $A$
to $B$ by fixing every element of $A\cap B$. The additional relations are
valid by reflexivity.
\end{proof}
In particular, the truth of $A\match_\tau B$ depends on the disjoint core
$(A\setminus B,B\setminus A)$, not on the choice of common elements
(manuscript~01).

\subsection{Signed vectors and transport feasibility}
For a disjoint pair $(U,V)$, define $d_i=1$ on $U$, $d_i=-1$ on $V$, and
$d_i=0$ elsewhere. Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:idealhall} gives a useful equivalent
formula:
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_k\gamma_k(\tau)u^k
  =\sum_{\substack{d\in\{-1,0,1\}^{E}\\d(E)=0\\
                          d(I)\ge0\ (I\in\J)}}
                      u^{|\{i:d_i=1\}|}.
 \label{gam:eq:signed}
\end{equation}
Thus the coefficients count balanced signed labelings whose cumulative sum
on every ideal is nonnegative. This is not a count of integer flows with
multiplicity: distinct realizations of the same signed labeling still
contribute once.

For a chain, these are Dyck words with zero symbols inserted. For a general
preorder, ideals replace initial intervals. Equivalent elements are tested
only together in the ideal inequalities, but their signs remain individually
visible in \eqref{gam:eq:signed}. This distinction will produce the block
multiplicities in Section~\ref{gam:sec:block}.

\subsection{Recovering the doubled-graph identity}
\begin{lemma}[The doubled graph's demand body; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:lem:fullgraph}
After identifying right coordinates with $E$,
$\Qt\cap\Z^E=\mathcal R_{G_\tau}$. The analogous equality holds for real
nonnegative vectors satisfying the same inequalities.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The neighborhood of $T_R$ in $G_\tau$ is $(\down T)_L$. If $x\in\Qt$,
nonnegativity and the ideal inequality for $\down T$ give
$x(T)\le x(\down T)\le|\down T|$. Conversely, when the demand inequalities
hold, take $T=I$ for an ideal; then $\down I=I$, so $x(I)\le |I|$.
\end{proof}
[Merge note: this is Part~I's Lemma~\ref{lem:draconian} with the shores of
$G_\tau$ exchanged.] Proposition~\ref{gam:prop:gradedinput} therefore yields
\begin{equation}
 h_\tau(t)=p_\tau(t),\qquad
 H_\tau(\zv)=\sum_{x\in\Qt\cap\Z^E}z_{\supp(x)}
     =\sum_{A\match_\tau B}z_B,
 \label{gam:eq:doubled}
\end{equation}
where $p_\tau=p_{G_\tau}$ and the sets $A$ and $B$ need not be disjoint as
subsets of $E$ (manuscript~01's Proposition ``Relation-graph
realization''). The univariate identity is already contained in the cited
support-enumerator and root-polytope results; it is Part~II's
Remark~\ref{htwo:rem:doubled}. Here it follows from the weaker total-count
input by Boolean inversion.

For a matchable pair, set
\[
 U=A\setminus B,\quad V=B\setminus A,\quad W=A\cap B.
\]
Cancellation says exactly that $(U,V)\in\Tset(\tau)$, while $W$ is an arbitrary
subset of $E\setminus(U\cup V)$. Conversely, every such triple gives
$A=U\cup W$, $B=V\cup W$ and a matchable pair. Hence
\[
 \sum_{A\match_\tau B}z_B
   =\sum_{(U,V)\in\Tset(\tau)}z_V
        \sum_{W\subseteq E\setminus(U\cup V)}z_W,
\]
which is a second derivation of Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:main}. This proof and the
support-fibre proof share the classical counting input but not the same
preorder reduction; manuscript~01 stresses that the second route is not an
independent replacement for the classical input.

\subsection{Cancellation characterizes transitivity}
\begin{proposition}[Manuscript~06]\label{gam:prop:canceliff}
Let $R$ be reflexive on a finite set. Common-support cancellation holds for
all equally sized pairs if and only if $R$ is transitive.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The transitive case is Corollary~\ref{gam:cor:cancel}. If $R$ is not
transitive, choose $aRb$ and $bRc$ with $aRc$ false. Reflexivity ensures
that $a,b,c$ are distinct. The pair
\[
 A=\{a,b\},\qquad B=\{b,c\}
\]
is matched by $a\mapsto b$ and $b\mapsto c$. After deleting the common
vertex $b$, the pair $(\{a\},\{c\})$ is not matchable. Thus cancellation
fails already on a two-edge path.
\end{proof}

\section{Boolean orbits and a recovered Ehrhart interpretation}\label{gam:gv:sec:orbits}
\noindent\emph{(Manuscript~01 only.)}
\begin{theorem}[Boolean orbits]\label{gam:gv:thm:orbits}
Let $\mathscr M_\tau$ be the set of compatible ordered pairs $(A,B)$,
$A\match_\tau B$, graded by $|B|$. For each $v\in E$, define a map $T_v$ as
follows: if $v$ belongs to exactly one of $A,B$, leave the pair unchanged;
otherwise toggle its membership in both sets. Then each $T_v$ is a
well-defined involution of $\mathscr M_\tau$, the involutions commute, and
their orbits are indexed by the disjoint compatible pairs $(U,V)$. The orbit
of a core of size $|U|=|V|=k$ is a Boolean family of dimension $n-2k$, with
rank polynomial $t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Each toggle preserves the disjoint core, so
Corollary~\ref{gam:cor:cancel} proves that it preserves compatibility.
Toggling twice returns the original pair. Different toggles act on different
element memberships and commute. For fixed core $(U,V)$, the common subset
$C$ can be chosen arbitrarily in the complement of $U\cup V$; the toggles
there add or remove its elements. The resulting orbit consists of all
$2^{n-2k}$ choices, and $|B|=|V|+|C|$.
\end{proof}
This is an explicit combinatorial orbit interpretation of the gamma
coefficients. It is an action on the \emph{support-pair model}, not an
explicit action on $\Qt\cap\Z^E$. The counting bridge supplies equal
support-fiber cardinalities, but transporting the toggles to lattice points
naturally would require an additional bijection. No such bijection is
claimed here (compare Research question~\ref{gam:q:orbits}).

\subsection{A recovered Ehrhart interpretation}
Let $B_{\widehat{G_\tau}}$ be the augmented edge polytope of the relation
graph. Its dimension is $2n$: its connected graph has $2n+2$ vertices, and
the sums on the two shores each equal one. Combining
\eqref{htwo:eq:OTinput} and \eqref{gam:eq:doubled} gives
\begin{equation}
 h^*(B_{\widehat{G_\tau}};t)=h_\tau(t).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:rootrecovered}
\end{equation}
This is a geometric consequence of the counting proof, not an identification
of $h_\tau$ with $h^*(\Qt;t)$. Root-polytope interpretations of support
enumerators are already part of the primary literature \cite{DHLTW}; the
importance here is the additional explicit gamma interpretation. [Merge
note: $B_{\widehat{G_\tau}}$ is Part~I's $B_\tau$ of~\eqref{eq:B} with its two
coordinate blocks exchanged, a lattice isomorphism, so
\eqref{gam:gv:eq:rootrecovered} is Part~I's Proposition~\ref{prop:known}.]

In particular,
\begin{align}
 \Vol(B_{\widehat{G_\tau}})
 &=|\Qt\cap\Z^E|
   =\sum_k\gamma_k(\tau)2^{n-2k},\label{gam:gv:eq:volume}\\
 |mB_{\widehat{G_\tau}}\cap\Z^{2n+2}|
 &=\sum_{j=0}^n [t^j]h_\tau(t)
           \binom{m+2n-j}{2n}\qquad(m\in\N).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:ehrhart}
\end{align}
The lattice in the second line is restricted automatically to the affine
span of the dilation. These are standard consequences of the Ehrhart
numerator identity. A $2n$-dimensional root-polytope realization does not,
by itself, settle the flag $n$-dimensional simplicial-polytope question.
(Manuscript~01 writes $\operatorname{NVol}$ for the normalized volume, which
is Part~I's $\Vol$ of~\eqref{eq:vol}.)

\section{An exact classification for balanced bipartite graphs}\label{gam:sec:graph}
For reflexive relations, failure of transitivity is visible in two
coefficients of the matching-support polynomial. This is stronger than
merely exhibiting a nonpalindromic counterexample. This section is
manuscript~06's.

\begin{lemma}[Near-perfect supports detect reachability]\label{gam:lem:reach}
Let $R$ be reflexive on $E$. For $a,b\in E$, there is a perfect matching
from $E\setminus\{a\}$ to $E\setminus\{b\}$ using $R$ if and only if
$b\,\overline R\,a$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
When $a=b$, diagonal edges give the matching and reachability is reflexive.
Assume $a\ne b$. A matching is a bijection
$f:E\setminus\{a\}\to E\setminus\{b\}$ with $iRf(i)$. Draw its edges as
arrows on $E$. The vertex $b$ has one outgoing arrow and no incoming arrow;
$a$ has one incoming arrow and no outgoing arrow; every other vertex has
one of each. Starting at $b$ and following arrows must reach $a$. It cannot
enter a directed cycle: the first cycle vertex entered would have two
incoming arrows, unless the path started on the cycle, which is impossible
at $b$. Thus there is an $R$-path from $b$ to $a$.

Conversely, choose a simple directed path
$b=x_0Rx_1R\cdots Rx_\ell=a$. Match $x_i$ to $x_{i+1}$ along the path, and
match every vertex outside the path to itself. These edges match precisely
$E\setminus\{a\}$ onto $E\setminus\{b\}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:graphintro}]
Matchable singleton pairs are the edges of $G_R$, so $[t]p_R=|R|$.
Pairs of size $n-1$ are indexed uniquely by their omitted labels $(a,b)$.
Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:reach} makes their number $|\overline R|$, proving
\eqref{gam:eq:endpoints} and \eqref{gam:eq:defect}.

Transitivity is equivalent to cancellation by
Proposition~\ref{gam:prop:canceliff}. Under transitivity, the decomposition
$(A,B)\leftrightarrow(U,V,W)$ in Section~\ref{gam:sec:cancel}, used directly on
\eqref{gam:eq:pR}, gives
\[
 p_R(t)=\sum_{(U,V)\in\Tset(R)}t^{|U|}(1+t)^{n-2|U|}.
\]
This implication uses no demand or root-polytope identity. Thus transitivity
implies gamma-positivity, which implies palindromicity and hence equality of
the coefficients of degrees $1$ and $n-1$. If those coefficients are equal,
then $|\overline R|=|R|$. Since $R\subseteq\overline R$ and both sets are
finite, $R=\overline R$; that is, $R$ is transitive. All five conditions are
equivalent.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Balanced graphs with a perfect matching]\label{gam:cor:balanced}
Let $H=(X\sqcup Y,F)$ be a bipartite graph with $|X|=|Y|=n$, admitting a
perfect matching. Label the shores $x_1,\ldots,x_n$ and $y_1,\ldots,y_n$ so
that $x_iy_i$ are the edges of a chosen perfect matching, and define
$iRj$ by $x_iy_j\in F$. The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt]
\item the perfectly matchable set polynomial $p_H(t)$ is palindromic;
\item $p_H(t)$ is gamma-positive;
\item the relation $R$ is a preorder.
\end{enumerate}
These conditions do not depend on the chosen perfect matching. They are also
equivalent to palindromicity, or to gamma-positivity, of the demand support
polynomial $F_H(t)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The chosen matching supplies every diagonal relation, so
Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:graphintro} applies. The polynomial $p_H$ is unchanged by
relabeling or by a different choice of distinguished matching. Thus the
criterion must hold for every such choice whenever it holds for one.
Finally, Proposition~\ref{gam:prop:gradedinput} gives $F_H=p_H$.
\end{proof}
This answers the palindromicity/gamma-positivity part of the
support-enumerator classification question in \cite[Problem~5.3]{DHLTW}
for the balanced perfect-matching class. It is not a classification for
unbalanced graphs or for balanced graphs without perfect matchings, nor is
it a real-rootedness criterion. The matching-theoretic ingredients are
standard; the explicit endpoint-defect interpretation and its application
here are supplied with proofs, without a separate claim of absolute
historical priority.

\begin{example}[A missing transitive edge]\label{gam:ex:nontrans}
Let $E=\{a,b,c\}$, with all loops and just the additional relations $aRb$
and $bRc$. There are five related pairs, but its transitive closure has six.
Directly,
\[
 p_R(t)=1+5t+6t^2+t^3.
\]
The defect $6-5=1$ detects the single missing pair $(a,c)$. Adding that pair
produces the three-element chain, with
\[
 p_{\overline R}(t)=1+6t+6t^2+t^3=(1+t)^3+3t(1+t).
\]
This example illustrates why one cannot simply drop transitivity in the
preorder theorem. It also shows why using a Hasse diagram instead of the
full order relation changes the polynomial.
\end{example}
[Merge note, not in manuscript~06: this is Part~I's nonpalindromic example
of subsection~\ref{gam:sec:reflexiveex}. There $D_i$ is the set of $j$ with
$jRi$, so $D_1=\{1\}$, $D_2=\{1,2\}$, $D_3=\{2,3\}$ is the relation above with
$(a,b,c)=(1,2,3)$, and by Proposition~\ref{gam:prop:gradedinput} its
draconian support counts $(1,5,6,1)$ are the coefficients of $p_R$:
$(1,|R|,|\overline R|,1)$. Part~I's explanation of what fails (the reduction
to ideal differences) is Proposition~\ref{gam:prop:canceliff} in another
form. The endpoint formula~\eqref{gam:eq:endpoints} and the equivalence of
palindromicity with transitivity were also checked by the batch-44 dossier
on all $4{,}165$ reflexive relations with $1\le n\le4$; that check is not
shipped.]

\section{Positive formulas for equivalence-class blowups}\label{gam:sec:block}
A preorder is a poset of equivalence classes, each carrying a positive
integer size. The signed-vector formula compresses the gamma polynomial to
this quotient without sacrificing positivity.

Let $P$ be the quotient poset of $\tau$, with blocks $E_v$ of sizes
$s_v\ge1$, and let $N=\sum_{v\in P}s_v$. Ideals of $\tau$ are exactly unions
of blocks whose indices form ideals of $P$. Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal D(P,\mathbf s)=
 \left\{d\in\Z^P:
 \begin{array}{l}
 |d_v|\le s_v\text{ for all }v,\quad \sum_vd_v=0,\\
 \sum_{v\in I}d_v\ge0\text{ for every }I\in\mathcal J(P)
 \end{array}\right\},\qquad
 D(d)=\sum_v\max(d_v,0).
 \label{gam:eq:blockdomain}
\end{equation}
For $0\le r\le s$, put
\begin{equation}
 B_{s,r}(u)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(s-r)/2\rfloor}
       \frac{s!}{j!(j+r)!(s-r-2j)!}\,u^j.
 \label{gam:eq:localB}
\end{equation}
All these coefficients are multinomial integers.

\begin{theorem}[Quotient-block formula; manuscript~06]\label{gam:thm:blocks}
For every finite preorder with quotient data $(P,\mathbf s)$,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_{d\in\mathcal D(P,\mathbf s)}
                   u^{D(d)}\prod_{v\in P}B_{s_v,|d_v|}(u).}
 \label{gam:eq:blocks}
\end{equation}
This applies to arbitrary quotient height and arbitrary nontrivial block
sizes.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a transport pair $(U,V)$, put
$a_v=|U\cap E_v|$, $b_v=|V\cap E_v|$, and $d_v=a_v-b_v$.
Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:idealhall} says that the pair is matchable exactly when its
block differences lie in \eqref{gam:eq:blockdomain}: all labels in a block have
the same comparisons to every other block, and all ideals are block unions.

For fixed $d_v$, let $j_v=\min(a_v,b_v)$. The counts in the block are then
$j_v+|d_v|$ of one sign, $j_v$ of the other, and
$s_v-|d_v|-2j_v$ zeros. The number of assignments of these signs to the
labeled elements is
\[
 \frac{s_v!}{j_v!(j_v+|d_v|)!(s_v-|d_v|-2j_v)!}.
\]
Every assignment has the same feasibility status, because the ideal
conditions depend only on $d$. The common cardinality of the two supports is
\[
 |U|=\sum_v\bigl(\max(d_v,0)+j_v\bigr)=D(d)+\sum_vj_v.
\]
Multiplying the local counts and summing first over $j_v$, then over $d$,
proves the formula.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A companion formula in the ordinary basis}
For $0\le r\le s$, define
\begin{equation}
 A_{s,r}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{s-r}\binom{s}{j+r}\binom{s}{j}t^j.
 \label{gam:eq:localA}
\end{equation}
Extracting the coefficient of $z^r$ in the Laurent identity
\[
 (1+z)^s(1+t/z)^s=(1+t+z+t/z)^s
\]
gives the positive gamma expansion
\begin{equation}
 A_{s,r}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor(s-r)/2\rfloor}
       \frac{s!}{j!(j+r)!(s-r-2j)!}\,t^j(1+t)^{s-r-2j}.
 \label{gam:eq:Aexpand}
\end{equation}
To see the right-hand count explicitly, choose $j+r$ copies of $z$, $j$
copies of $t/z$, and $s-r-2j$ copies of $1+t$ in the second expression.
The first expression yields \eqref{gam:eq:localA}.

Since $\sum_v|d_v|=2D(d)$, substitution into
Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:blocks} gives
\begin{equation}
 h_\tau(t)=\sum_{d\in\mathcal D(P,\mathbf s)}
                    t^{D(d)}\prod_{v\in P}A_{s_v,|d_v|}(t).
 \label{gam:eq:blockh}
\end{equation}
No rational functions remain in this identity. Each summand has center
$N/2$ because its degree after the shift is $N-D(d)$ and its smallest
possible exponent is $D(d)$.

\begin{example}[A nontrivial height-two blowup]\label{gam:ex:twoblocks}
Take two equivalence classes of size two, with every member of the first
below every member of the second. The feasible differences are $(r,-r)$,
$r=0,1,2$. Since
\[
 B_{2,0}=1+2u,\qquad B_{2,1}=2,\qquad B_{2,2}=1,
\]
we get
\[
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=(1+2u)^2+4u+u^2=1+8u+5u^2,
\]
and therefore
\[
 h_\tau(t)=1+12t+27t^2+12t^3+t^4.
\]
The quotient has height two, but the preorder is not a height-two poset.
It lies beyond the scope of Part~II's height-two theorem.
The example also exposes the importance of counting ordered support pairs
inside an equivalence class.
\end{example}

\subsection{Algorithmic consequence and its encoding qualification}
The empty quotient gives $\Gamma_\tau=1$. With $r=|P|\ge1$ fixed,
\eqref{gam:eq:blocks} computes $\Gamma_\tau$ in a number of integer
arithmetic operations polynomial in the expanded size $N$. Enumerate the
ideals of the $r$-element quotient once. Enumerate $d_1,\ldots,d_{r-1}$ and
determine $d_r$ from the zero-sum equation, rejecting out-of-range values and
failed ideal inequalities. There are at most $(2N+1)^{r-1}$ candidates. For
each accepted candidate multiply the local polynomials, using ordinary
polynomial multiplication.

For example, a coarse bound after local precomputation is
\[
 O\!\left(2^r r^2+(2N+1)^{r-1}(r2^r+rN^2)\right)
\]
arithmetic operations. This is an arithmetic, not bit-operation, estimate.
It is polynomial for fixed quotient size when $N$ measures the expanded
input. If the block sizes are encoded in binary, the bound is only
pseudopolynomial in that compressed input; no stronger complexity assertion
is intended.

\subsection{The profile form of the block formula (manuscript~01)}
\label{gam:gv:sec:blocks}
Manuscript~01 proves an equivalent block formula with a different
parametrization and proof. Let $P=\tau/{\sim}$ be the quotient poset, where
$u\sim v$ means both $u\le_\tau v$ and $v\le_\tau u$. Index its equivalence
classes by $1,\ldots,b$, with positive sizes $m_1,\ldots,m_b$. Relations
between two classes are uniform: every element of class $i$ lies below every
element of class $j$ precisely when $i\le_P j$. Set $n=\sum_i m_i$.

For a disjoint pair $(U,V)$, let
$\alpha_i=|U\cap E_i|$ and $\beta_i=|V\cap E_i|$. These profiles satisfy
\[
 \alpha_i,\beta_i\in\N,\qquad \alpha_i+\beta_i\le m_i,
 \qquad \sum_i\alpha_i=\sum_i\beta_i.
\]
For $T\subseteq[b]$, set
$\down_P T=\{i:i\le_P j\text{ for some }j\in T\}$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact block formula; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:thm:blocks}
Let $\mathcal A(P,\mathbf m)$ consist of the profiles above which also satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j\in T}\beta_j\le
        \sum_{i\in\down_P T}\alpha_i
                   \qquad(T\subseteq[b]).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:blockhall}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\displaystyle
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal A(P,\mathbf m)}
   \left(\prod_{i=1}^b
       \frac{m_i!}{\alpha_i!\,\beta_i!\,(m_i-\alpha_i-\beta_i)!}\right)
         u^{\sum_i\beta_i}.}
 \label{gam:gv:eq:blockformula}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A matching covering the chosen receivers can use exactly the selected
sources in predecessor classes. For the set of all selected receivers in
classes $T$, Hall's inequality is \eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockhall}. These inequalities
are also sufficient: any subset of selected receivers uses some class set
$T$ and has at most $\sum_{j\in T}\beta_j$ members; its neighbors are all
selected sources in $\down_P T$. Thus Hall's criterion applies to the
individual selected vertices. Equality of the total source and receiver
counts makes a receiver-covering matching a bijection.

For a fixed feasible profile, the choices of disjoint sources and receivers
in class $i$ number
$\binom{m_i}{\alpha_i}\binom{m_i-\alpha_i}{\beta_i}$.
Compatibility depends only on the profiles, not on the chosen labels,
because class-to-class relations are uniform. Multiplying these counts and
summing the feasible profiles gives \eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockformula}.
\end{proof}
This is a closed finite formula, not a claim that the quotient poset alone
determines $h_\tau$: the multiplicities $m_i$ are indispensable. Replacing
the classes by singletons loses both the profile range and the multinomial
weights. [Merge note: grouping the profiles of~\eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockformula}
by $d_i=\alpha_i-\beta_i$ and $j_i=\min(\alpha_i,\beta_i)$ gives
\eqref{gam:eq:blocks}; \eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockhall} is Hall's condition on
receivers, whereas \eqref{gam:eq:blockdomain} is the ideal form of
Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:idealhall}.]

\emph{Algorithmic consequence (manuscript~01).} For fixed $b$, there are at most
\begin{equation}
 \prod_{i=1}^b\binom{m_i+2}{2}=O(n^{2b})
 \label{gam:gv:eq:profilestates}
\end{equation}
profiles before imposing equal totals. Testing a profile by
\eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockhall} takes at most $2^b$ subset inequalities, each involving
at most $b$ additions after the lower closures are precomputed. Therefore
\eqref{gam:gv:eq:blockformula} computes the entire gamma polynomial in polynomial
time in the \emph{expanded cardinality} $n$ for a fixed number of classes.
This is not a bound polynomial in the bit length of binary-encoded
$m_i$, and it is not a polynomial bound when $b$ varies with $n$.
The weights and their sums use exact integers. The universal gamma bound
is at most $3^n$, since a disjoint pair assigns each element to source,
receiver, or neither, so the output coefficients have $O(n)$ bits. Large
block sizes therefore enlarge the enumerated state space, but do not create
uncontrolled coefficient bit lengths relative to expanded $n$.

\begin{example}[Examples beyond Part~II's domain; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:ex:beyond}
For a chain of three quotient classes of sizes $(2,1,2)$, the formula gives
\[
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=1+12u+17u^2,
 \qquad
 h_\tau(t)=1+17t+63t^2+63t^3+17t^4+t^5.
\]
The quotient has height three and two classes are nontrivial. Both
restrictions excluded by Part~II's height-two-poset proof fail here.

For two incomparable classes of sizes $2$ and $1$ below a class of size $3$,
\[
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=1+17u+36u^2+7u^3,
\]
\[
 h_\tau(t)=1+23t+119t^2+201t^3+119t^4+23t^5+t^6.
\]
Its comparability graph has a perfect matching. Hence
$h_\tau(-1)=-7$, agreeing with Corollary~\ref{gam:gv:cor:derivative}.
The companion program checks these values both by direct ideal-inequality
lattice enumeration and by the block formula.
\end{example}

\section{Monotonicity, duality, extremal coefficients, and basic families}\label{gam:sec:extremal}
The transport interpretation gives comparisons between different preorders
without comparing their lattice points individually.

\begin{theorem}[Monotonicity and products; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:thm:monotone}
Suppose $\tau$ and $\sigma$ are preorders on the same set and
$\le_\tau\,\subseteq\,\le_\sigma$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_k(\tau)\le\gamma_k(\sigma)\quad\text{for every }k.
 \label{gam:eq:monotonicity}
\end{equation}
More precisely $h_\sigma-h_\tau$ has a nonnegative expansion in the
$n$-centered gamma basis. If the relations differ, then
$\gamma_1(\tau)<\gamma_1(\sigma)$, so the two gamma vectors and the two
support polynomials differ (manuscript~01). Moreover, for disjoint unions
and opposites,
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_{\tau\sqcup\sigma}(u)=\Gamma_\tau(u)\Gamma_\sigma(u),
 \qquad \Gamma_{\tau^*}(u)=\Gamma_\tau(u).
 \label{gam:eq:productdual}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Every matching allowed by $\tau$ is allowed by $\sigma$, so
$\Tset_k(\tau)\subseteq\Tset_k(\sigma)$. For $k=1$, these pairs are exactly
the distinct-element relations; since both preorders contain all diagonal
relations, a proper inclusion adds at least one such pair. Subtract the two
expansions in~\eqref{gam:eq:main}. In a disjoint union, matchings cannot
cross components. Thus each transport support pair splits uniquely into a
transport pair in each component, and cardinalities add. Finally, duality
sends $(U,V)$ to $(V,U)$ and reverses a witnessing bijection.
\end{proof}
The corresponding duality for $h$ was already proved in \cite{DHLTW}; this
is a transport-support explanation of it, not a first proof claim. The
monotonicity also implies coefficientwise monotonicity of $h$, since every
gamma-basis polynomial has nonnegative coefficients; that weaker statement
also follows from $\Qt\subseteq\mathcal Q_\sigma$, because every
$\sigma$-ideal is a $\tau$-ideal. Manuscript~01 notes that the theorem is
stronger: the difference remains nonnegative in the more restrictive gamma
basis.

\begin{theorem}[Multivariate complement-duality; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:thm:duality}
Let $\tau^*$ be the opposite preorder. Then
\begin{equation}
 H_\tau(\zv)=z_E\, H_{\tau^*}(\zv^{-1}).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:duality}
\end{equation}
In particular $\gamma_k(\tau)=\gamma_k(\tau^*)$ and $h_\tau=h_{\tau^*}$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The map $(U,V)\mapsto(V,U)$ is a bijection from
$\Tset_k(\tau)$ to $\Tset_k(\tau^*)$, since a compatible bijection
can be inverted. In the term of \eqref{gam:eq:multi} indexed by $(V,U)$ for
$\tau^*$, invert all variables and multiply by $z_E$. With
$F=E\setminus(U\cup V)$, the term becomes
\[
 z_E\,z_U^{-1}\prod_{w\in F}(1+z_w^{-1})
   =z_V\prod_{w\in F}(1+z_w),
\]
which is exactly the corresponding term for $\tau$. The Laurent expression
simplifies to a polynomial. The univariate conclusions also follow directly
by swapping the disjoint pairs.
\end{proof}
Equivalently, the number of lattice points of exact support $S$ for $\tau$
equals the number of exact support $E\setminus S$ for $\tau^*$. This does
\emph{not} assert that $H_\tau$ itself is invariant under complementing the
support. For the chain $1<2$,
$H_\tau=1+z_1+2z_2+z_1z_2$, which illustrates the distinction. [Merge note:
the univariate identity $h_\tau=h_{\tau^*}$ is Conjecture~5.4 of~\cite{AC},
settled by~\cite[Theorem~1.3]{DHLTW}; see Part~I,
Section~\ref{sec:consequences}.]

\begin{proposition}[Disjoint unions; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:prop:products}
For preorders on disjoint sets with no cross-relations,
\[
 H_{\tau\sqcup\sigma}=H_\tau H_\sigma,\qquad
 h_{\tau\sqcup\sigma}=h_\tau h_\sigma,\qquad
 \Gamma_{\tau\sqcup\sigma}=\Gamma_\tau\Gamma_\sigma.
\]
Adjoining an isolated element multiplies $h$ by $1+t$ and leaves
$\Gamma$ unchanged.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The defining ideal inequalities separate, giving a product of polytopes.
Alternatively a compatible pair and its matching separate into the two
ground sets, with equal source and receiver cardinalities within each.
Disjoint compatible cores therefore multiply as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The first gamma coefficient; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:cor:gammaone}
For every preorder,
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_0(\tau)=1,\qquad
 \gamma_1(\tau)=\#\{(u,v):u\ne v,\ u\le_\tau v\}=|\tau|-n.
 \label{gam:eq:gammaone}
\end{equation}
In particular, $h_1(\tau)=|\tau|=\sum_{v\in E}|\down\{v\}|$. Equivalent
distinct elements contribute in both directions to $\gamma_1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The $k=1$ transport pairs are exactly the non-diagonal related pairs. The
coefficient of $t$ in \eqref{gam:eq:main} is $n+\gamma_1$. Directly
(manuscript~01): for a fixed single-element support $\{v\}$, the admissible
positive coordinate values are $1,\ldots,|\down\{v\}|$, because every ideal
containing $v$ contains its principal ideal. [Merge note: this is Part~I's
formula~\eqref{eq:h1}.]
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The second gamma coefficient; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:prop:low}
Let $D_2(\tau)$ count unordered pairs of directed distinct-element relations
with four distinct endpoints. Let $R_{2,2}(\tau)$ count ordered disjoint pairs
$(U,V)$ with $|U|=|V|=2$ and every relation $u\le_\tau v$, $u\in U$, $v\in V$,
present. Then
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_2(\tau)=D_2(\tau)-R_{2,2}(\tau).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:g2}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Every pair counted by $D_2$ is a matching witnessing some disjoint
$(U,V)$ of size two. Such a support pair has two witnessing bijections
exactly when all four possible source-to-receiver relations exist; otherwise
it has one. Subtracting the number of doubled pairs gives \eqref{gam:gv:eq:g2}.
\end{proof}
[Merge note: for a height-two poset $P_G$ this is Part~II's
$\gamma_2=m_2(G)-c_4(G)$ of Corollary~\ref{htwo:cor:small}: $D_2=m_2(G)$, and
$R_{2,2}$ counts the four-cycles.]

\begin{theorem}[Sharp coefficientwise bounds; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:thm:bounds}
For every preorder on $n$ elements and $0\le k\le\lfloor n/2\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}
 0\le\gamma_k(\tau)\le
     \frac{n!}{k!^2(n-2k)!}=\binom n{2k}\binom{2k}k,
 \qquad
 \binom n j\le[t^j]h_\tau(t)\le\binom n j^2\quad(0\le j\le n).
 \label{gam:eq:universalbound}
\end{equation}
For an ordinary poset the sharper bound is
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_k(\tau)\le\binom{n}{2k}C_k,
 \qquad C_k=\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{2k}{k}.
 \label{gam:eq:posetbound}
\end{equation}
The upper bounds are attained simultaneously for all $k$ by, respectively,
the universal equivalence preorder $\mathbf E_n$ and a total order $L_n$.
The antichain attains $\gamma_k=0$ for all $k\ge1$, and the lower bounds
on~$h$. For $n\ge2$, equality of the complete gamma vector with the upper
or lower extremal vector characterizes the corresponding extremal preorder,
and equality with the chain vector characterizes a chain among posets
(manuscript~01).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
There are $\binom{n}{k}\binom{n-k}{k}=n!/(k!^2(n-2k)!)$ ordered pairs of
disjoint $k$-element sets, which gives the first upper bound. Every such
pair is matchable in the universal equivalence preorder. The antichain
relation is contained in every preorder, and every preorder is contained in
$\mathbf E_n$; Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:monotone} gives the bounds on $h$ from
\eqref{gam:eq:antichainh} and~\eqref{gam:eq:universal} below.
Alternatively, the unit cube is contained in $\Qt$, while $\Qt$ is contained
in the full simplex $\{x\ge0:x(E)\le n\}$ (manuscript~01).

Every finite poset has a linear extension, obtained recursively by removing
a minimal element. Its relation is a subset of that total order. It is
therefore enough, by Theorem~\ref{gam:thm:monotone}, to compute the chain case.
Choose the $2k$ nonzero positions in the signed-vector formula
\eqref{gam:eq:signed}. In their increasing order, the signs must have total zero
and nonnegative partial sums. There are $C_k$ such words: among the
$\binom{2k}{k}$ balanced sign words, reflection up to the first negative
partial sum bijects the bad ones with words having $k+1$ plus signs and
$k-1$ minus signs. Hence the good count is
$\binom{2k}{k}-\binom{2k}{k-1}=C_k$. This proves
\eqref{gam:eq:posetbound} and sharpness. In an antichain a disjoint nonempty
transport pair cannot be matched, since the only relation edges are loops.

The characterizations follow from $\gamma_1$: zero means that no
off-diagonal relations exist, $n(n-1)$ means that all of them exist, and
$\binom n2$ for a poset forces total comparability.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of the chain count, by initial segments (manuscript~01)]
Manuscript~01 states $\gamma_k(L_n)=\binom n{2k}C_k$ as its Proposition
``The chain gamma vector''. Fix the $2k$ elements in $U\cup V$, and list
them in increasing order. A compatible matching exists exactly when every
initial segment contains at least as many elements of $U$ as of $V$.
Necessity holds because a target must be matched to an earlier source. For
sufficiency, pair the $i$th source with the $i$th target; the
initial-segment inequalities force the source to precede the target. Among
the $\binom{2k}k$ words with $k$ source letters and $k$ target letters, the
words whose running source-minus-target balance never becomes negative are
counted by $\binom{2k}k-\binom{2k}{k-1}=C_k$; the subtraction follows by
reflecting the prefix ending at the first negative balance. Multiply by the
choice of $U\cup V$. Relation-monotonicity under a linear extension gives
the bound.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Explicit consistency checks from classical families}
For an antichain, $\Qt=[0,1]^n$, so
\begin{equation}
 h_\tau(t)=(1+t)^n,\qquad \Gamma_\tau(u)=1.
 \label{gam:eq:antichainh}
\end{equation}
For a total order, the preceding proof gives
\begin{equation}
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}\binom n{2k}C_k u^k.
 \label{gam:eq:chaingamma}
\end{equation}
This agrees with the classical Narayana specialization for a chain,
\begin{equation}
 [t^j]h_{L_n}(t)
  =\binom n j^2-\binom n{j-1}\binom n{j+1}
  =\frac1{n+1}\binom{n+1}j\binom{n+1}{j+1},
 \label{gam:gv:eq:narayana}
\end{equation}
recorded among the elementary families of \cite{AC}; the identity also
follows by expanding \eqref{gam:eq:chaingamma}. Here a binomial coefficient
with lower index outside its usual range is zero. Manuscript~01 gives a
direct proof using~\eqref{gam:eq:doubled}: if the two $j$-subsets are
$A=\{a_1<\cdots<a_j\}$ and $B=\{b_1<\cdots<b_j\}$, compatibility is
equivalent to $a_i\le b_i$ for all $i$, or to $|A\cap[r]|\ge|B\cap[r]|$ for
every $r$. There are $\binom n j^2$ pairs in total. For a violating pair,
exchange the two memberships on the first prefix for which the target count
exceeds the source count by one. This is a bijection to a pair of subsets of
sizes $j+1$ and $j-1$; the inverse uses the first prefix with source surplus
one. This proves the first equality, and factorial simplification proves
the second. Thus~\eqref{gam:gv:eq:narayana} gives sharp ordinary coefficient
bounds for posets as well. These classical formulas are checks, not novelty
claims.

For the universal equivalence preorder $\mathbf E_n$, the only ideals are
$\varnothing$ and $E$, so $\Qt=\{x\ge0:x(E)\le n\}$. Choosing a support of
size $j$ and then its positive coordinates gives
\begin{equation}
 h_{\mathbf E_n}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^n\binom nj^2t^j,
 \qquad
 \Gamma_{\mathbf E_n}(u)=\sum_{k=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}
                 \frac{n!}{k!^2(n-2k)!}u^k.
 \label{gam:eq:universal}
\end{equation}
For the first formula, subtract one on the chosen support; the number of
resulting nonnegative $j$-tuples of total at most $n-j$ is $\binom nj$.
The second follows directly from unrestricted disjoint transport pairs.
These are familiar special-case polynomials (Part~I's indiscrete preorder,
Section~\ref{sec:examples}); the point is their role as universal
coefficientwise bounds.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Four-element examples (manuscript~06). Each $h$ is
$1+h_1t+h_2t^2+h_1t^3+t^4$, and each $\Gamma$ is $1+\gamma_1u+\gamma_2u^2$.}
\label{gam:tab:examples}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrrr@{}}
\toprule
Preorder & $h_1$ & $h_2$ & $\gamma_1$ & $\gamma_2$\\
\midrule
Antichain & 4 & 6 & 0 & 0\\
Diamond poset & 9 & 18 & 5 & 2\\
Total order & 10 & 20 & 6 & 2\\
Two ordered blocks of size two & 12 & 27 & 8 & 5\\
Universal equivalence & 16 & 36 & 12 & 6\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{The octopus formula as an immediate specialization (manuscript~01)}
Consider $\ell$ pairwise incomparable singleton leaves below a root
equivalence class of size $n-\ell$. In a nonempty disjoint compatible pair,
a receiver cannot be a leaf, because its only predecessor outside the root
order is itself and sources and receivers are disjoint. Conversely, once
$V$ is any $k$-subset of the root, every $k$-subset $U\subseteq E\setminus V$
is compatible with it. Therefore
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_k=\binom{n-\ell}k\binom{n-k}k.
 \label{gam:gv:eq:octopus}
\end{equation}
This recovers the octopus formula attributed to Athanasiadis, Xiao, and Yan
and reproved bijectively by Menon \cite[Proposition~1.1]{Menon}. It is not a
new formula claimed here. The general theorem also applies when leaf blocks
have arbitrary positive size and when the quotient is not a star;
Section~\ref{gam:sec:block} gives the exact count without any unit-block
assumption.

\subsection{Recovery of the height-two matching-support formula}
\label{gam:sec:heighttwo}
If $\tau$ is a poset of height at most two, write its strict relations as
edges of a bipartite graph $G=(A\sqcup B,F)$ with $a<b$ on each edge.
Every nonempty transport pair has $U\subseteq A$ and $V\subseteq B$.
Therefore its union $U\cup V$ determines the ordered pair uniquely, and
\[
 \Gamma_\tau(u)=\sum_{\substack{W\subseteq A\sqcup B\\
                                  G[W]\text{ has a perfect matching}}}
                         u^{|W|/2}=p_G(u).
\]
This recovers Part~II's height-two result (Theorem~\ref{htwo:thm:main})
exactly. Beyond height two, the two signs of a transport pair need not
occupy fixed shores of the original poset, which is why a fixed
comparability-graph support count is no longer the right general
definition. In a preorder with equivalence classes, the union may also admit
several orientations; those are genuinely distinct ordered pairs and must
not be collapsed. [Merge note: Part~II's proof partitions a point by its
zeros on the minimal shore $A$ and its support on $B$
(Section~\ref{htwo:sec:gamma}); Lemma~\ref{gam:lem:fibre} partitions by
the whole zero set, which is what removes the height restriction.]

\section{Matching deficiency and the exact root at minus one}\label{gam:sec:deficiency}
Let $\Comp(\tau)$ denote the \emph{simple comparability graph}: its vertices
are $E$, and distinct $u,v$ are adjacent when $u\le_\tau v$ or
$v\le_\tau u$. Write $\nu(\Comp(\tau))$ for its maximum matching size.

\begin{theorem}[Degree and root multiplicity; manuscripts~06 and~01]\label{gam:thm:deficiency}
For every finite preorder,
\begin{equation}
 \deg\Gamma_\tau=\nu(\Comp(\tau)),\qquad
 \ordm_{t=-1}h_\tau(t)=n-2\nu(\Comp(\tau)).
 \label{gam:eq:deficiency}
\end{equation}
Furthermore $\gamma_k(\tau)>0$ exactly for $0\le k\le\nu(\Comp(\tau))$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A transport pair of size $k$ has a witnessing directed matching on $2k$
distinct vertices. Forgetting orientations gives a $k$-matching in
$\Comp(\tau)$, so $\deg\Gamma_\tau\le\nu(\Comp(\tau))$. Conversely, orient
each edge of an undirected matching in a permitted preorder direction. Its
tails and heads form a disjoint transport pair. Submatchings supply every
smaller size. Hence the degree is exactly $\nu=\nu(\Comp(\tau))$.

Factor the smallest power of $1+t$ from \eqref{gam:eq:main}:
\begin{equation}
 h_\tau(t)=(1+t)^{n-2\nu}
   \left(\gamma_\nu t^\nu+
    \sum_{k<\nu}\gamma_k t^k(1+t)^{2(\nu-k)}\right).
 \label{gam:eq:minusonefactor}
\end{equation}
The expression in parentheses has value $(-1)^\nu\gamma_\nu\ne0$ at $-1$.
Thus the displayed exponent is the exact multiplicity, not just a lower
bound.
\end{proof}
This reduces the gamma degree and the entire $-1$ multiplicity question to a
single ordinary maximum-matching problem, even when the remaining
coefficients are expensive to count. It uses the full comparability graph,
not merely the cover graph. The maximum matching here is an ordinary
matching in the \emph{undirected} comparability graph. It is not a maximum
matching in $G_\tau$, whose reflexive diagonal already supplies a perfect
matching of size $n$. Confusing these two graphs would destroy the degree
formula (manuscript~01). [Merge note: in height two, $\Comp(P_G)=G$, and
this is the multiplicity $n-2\nu(G)$ of Part~II's
Proposition~\ref{htwo:prop:roottransfer}.]

For example, a height-two star with $m$ edges has $n=m+1$ and maximum
matching size one. Its polynomial is
\[
 h_\tau(t)=(1+t)^{m-1}\bigl((1+t)^2+mt\bigr),
\]
so its exact multiplicity at $-1$ is $m-1$. A chain has matching number
$\lfloor n/2\rfloor$, giving multiplicity zero for even $n$ and one for odd
$n$. Equivalent vertices cause no exception: one may choose either
allowed direction when both are present.

\begin{corollary}[The leading Taylor coefficient at $-1$; manuscript~01]\label{gam:gv:cor:derivative}
Put $d=n-2\nu(\Comp(\tau))$. Then
\begin{equation}
 h_\tau^{(j)}(-1)=0\quad(0\le j<d),\qquad
 h_\tau^{(d)}(-1)=d!(-1)^{\nu(\Comp(\tau))}\gamma_{\nu(\Comp(\tau))}(\tau).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:derivative}
\end{equation}
For even $n$, the signed value $(-1)^{n/2}h_\tau(-1)$ is positive exactly
when $\Comp(\tau)$ has a perfect matching, and is zero otherwise.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{gam:eq:minusonefactor} identifies the first nonzero coefficient
of the Taylor expansion at $-1$. If $n$ is even, $d=0$ is equivalent to
$\nu=n/2$.
\end{proof}

\section{Induced restrictions and a deletion--differentiation law}\label{gam:gv:sec:deck}
\noindent\emph{(Manuscript~01 only.)}
For $W\subseteq E$, let $\tau|W$ be the induced preorder, retaining every
relation of $\tau$ whose endpoints lie in $W$. A matching between specified
subsets of $W$ is compatible in $\tau|W$ exactly when it is compatible in
$\tau$. In particular, no choice of an intermediate element outside $W$
is needed: the relation itself is already transitive.

\begin{theorem}[Induced-deck identities]\label{gam:gv:thm:deck}
For $0\le m\le n$ and $k\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{\substack{W\subseteq E\\|W|=m}}\gamma_k(\tau|W)
   =\binom{n-2k}{m-2k}\gamma_k(\tau),
 \label{gam:gv:eq:deckgamma}
\end{equation}
where the right side is zero when $2k>m$ or $2k>n$.
In particular, $\gamma_k(\tau)$ is the sum of the top gamma coefficients of its
induced $2k$-element subpreorders. If
\[
 D_\tau(t)=\sum_{v\in E}h_{\tau|(E\setminus\{v\})}(t),
\]
then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{(1-t)D_\tau(t)=n h_\tau(t)-2t h_\tau'(t).}
 \label{gam:gv:eq:deletiondifferential}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A fixed disjoint compatible pair of size $k$ is counted in the induced
preorder on $W$ exactly when its $2k$ endpoints lie in $W$. There are
$\binom{n-2k}{m-2k}$ such $W$, proving \eqref{gam:gv:eq:deckgamma}.
For one-element deletions this gives
\[
 D_\tau(t)=\sum_{2k\le n-1}(n-2k)\gamma_k(\tau)
                      t^k(1+t)^{n-1-2k}.
\]
On each gamma-basis term, direct differentiation yields
\[
 n\,t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}
 -2t\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm dt}\bigl(t^k(1+t)^{n-2k}\bigr)
 =(n-2k)(1-t)t^k(1+t)^{n-1-2k}.
\]
When $2k=n$, the left side is zero and that term is omitted on the right;
there is no evaluation of a negative exponent. Sum the identities.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Odd-cardinality reconstruction]\label{gam:gv:cor:odddeck}
If $n$ is odd, the multiset of polynomials of the one-element deletions
determines $h_\tau$. Explicitly, writing $D_\tau(t)=\sum_j d_jt^j$ with
$d_{-1}=d_n=0$, one has
\begin{equation}
 [t^j]h_\tau(t)=\frac{d_j-d_{j-1}}{n-2j}
                                      \qquad(0\le j\le n).
 \label{gam:gv:eq:oddreconstruction}
\end{equation}
For even $n$, the same formulas determine every coefficient except the
middle coefficient; the differential identity alone leaves one scalar free.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Compare coefficients in \eqref{gam:gv:eq:deletiondifferential}. For odd $n$, none
of the integers $n-2j$ is zero. For even $n$, exactly the middle one vanishes.
\end{proof}
The final statement is a limitation of this identity, not a claim that the
full combinatorial deletion deck never determines the middle coefficient.
This separates polynomial reconstruction from the harder reconstruction of
the preorder itself. (Here $d_j$ are coefficients of $D_\tau$, not the
deficiency $d$ of Corollary~\ref{gam:gv:cor:derivative}.)

\section{Support size as a mixture of centered binomials}\label{gam:sec:statistics}
The Boolean expansion provides a probability law without assuming a
canonical bijection on the original lattice points. Choose a lattice point
uniformly from $\Qt\cap\Z^E$, and let $L$ be its support size. All
probabilities below refer to this uniform distribution. This section is
manuscript~06's.

\begin{theorem}[Exact mixture and concentration]\label{gam:thm:statistics}
Let $\Gamma=\Gamma_\tau$ and $\nu=\deg\Gamma$. There is a mixing variable
$K\in\{0,\ldots,\nu\}$ with
\begin{equation}
 \PP(K=k)=\frac{\gamma_k4^{-k}}{\Gamma(1/4)}
 \label{gam:eq:mixweights}
\end{equation}
such that the law of $L$ is the mixture
\begin{equation}
 L\mid K=k\ \overset{\mathrm{law}}=\ k+\operatorname{Bin}(n-2k,1/2).
 \label{gam:eq:mixture}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\begin{align}
 |\Qt\cap\Z^E|&=2^n\Gamma(1/4),\label{gam:eq:pointcount}\\
 \EE L&=\frac n2,\qquad
 \Var L=\frac n4-\frac{\Gamma'(1/4)}{8\Gamma(1/4)},
 \label{gam:eq:variance}\\
 \frac{n-2\nu}{4}&\le\Var L\le\frac n4.\label{gam:eq:varbounds}
\end{align}
For $n\ge1$ and $a\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \PP\bigl(|L-n/2|\ge a\bigr)\le 2\exp(-2a^2/n).
 \label{gam:eq:tail}
\end{equation}
The upper variance bound is an equality exactly for antichains.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Evaluation of \eqref{gam:eq:main} at one gives \eqref{gam:eq:pointcount}. Divide
\eqref{gam:eq:main} by that value. The $k$th summand has total mass
$\gamma_k2^{n-2k}/h_\tau(1)$, which is \eqref{gam:eq:mixweights}, and its
normalized coefficients are those in \eqref{gam:eq:mixture}. Every component
has mean $n/2$ and variance $(n-2k)/4$. Thus the total variance is
$n/4-\EE K/2$. Since
\[
 \EE K=\frac{\Gamma'(1/4)}{4\Gamma(1/4)},
\]
we obtain \eqref{gam:eq:variance} and \eqref{gam:eq:varbounds}. Equality at the
upper bound means all $\gamma_k$ with $k>0$ vanish. By
Corollary~\ref{gam:cor:gammaone}, this is equivalent to having no non-diagonal
comparabilities.

For the tail bound, the centered moment generating function of the $k$th
component is $\cosh(s/2)^{n-2k}$. Since
$\cosh x\le\exp(x^2/2)$, this is at most $\exp(ns^2/8)$ for every real $s$.
The same bound holds after mixing. For $s>0$, Markov's inequality gives
\[
 \PP(L-n/2\ge a)\le\exp(-sa+ns^2/8).
\]
Choosing $s=4a/n$ gives $\exp(-2a^2/n)$, and the lower tail has the same
bound. Their sum proves \eqref{gam:eq:tail}. The elementary hyperbolic-cosine
inequality follows, for example, by integrating $\tanh x\le x$ for
$x\ge0$ and using evenness.
\end{proof}
The mixture law is not a representation of $L$ as a sum of a fixed family
of independent Bernoulli variables. In particular it does not imply
real-rootedness. Its value is that it gives exact central statistics and a
uniform tail bound directly from the proven gamma expansion. [Merge note,
not in manuscript~06: for a bipartite cactus $G$, Part~II's
\eqref{htwo:eq:variance} gives $\Var S=(n-2\nu)/4+\sum_j2/(4+\lambda_j)$ for
the same statistic ($S=L$), from $p_G(u)=\prod_j(1+\lambda_ju)$. Since then
$\Gamma'(1/4)/\Gamma(1/4)=\sum_j4\lambda_j/(4+\lambda_j)$,
formula~\eqref{gam:eq:variance} gives
$n/4-\sum_j\lambda_j/(2(4+\lambda_j))$, the same number, because
$\tfrac12-\lambda_j/(2(4+\lambda_j))=2/(4+\lambda_j)$. Here
$\Gamma(1/4)$ is $\Gamma_\tau$ evaluated at $1/4$, not Euler's Gamma
function.]

\section{Exact computations and reproducibility}\label{gam:sec:verification}
Both manuscripts supply a standard-library Python program that uses exact
integer arithmetic throughout its mathematical checks. Each is designed as a
cross-check of several logically different descriptions, not as an
unbounded theorem prover. The shipped files are:
